        "text": "# Probabilistic Inference in Language Models via Twisted Sequential Monte Carlo\n\nStephen Zhao $^{1\\,2\\,*}$ Rob Brekelmans $^{2\\,*}$ Alireza Makhzani $^{1\\,2\\,**}$ Roger Grosse $^{1\\,2\\,**}$ \n\n#### **Abstract**\n\nNumerous capability and safety techniques of Large Language Models (LLMs), including RLHF, automated red-teaming, prompt engineering, and infilling, can be cast as sampling from an unnormalized target distribution defined by a given reward or potential function over the full sequence. In this work, we leverage the rich toolkit of Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) for these probabilistic inference problems. In particular, we use learned twist functions to estimate the expected future value of the potential at each timestep, which enables us to focus inference-time computation on promising partial sequences. We propose a novel contrastive method for learning the twist functions, and establish connections with the rich literature of soft reinforcement learning. \n\nAs a complementary application of our twisted SMC framework, we present methods for evaluating the accuracy of language model inference techniques using novel bidirectional SMC bounds on the log partition function. These bounds can be used to estimate the KL divergence between the inference and target distributions in both directions. We apply our inference evaluation techniques to show that twisted SMC is effective for sampling undesirable outputs from a pretrained model (a useful component of harmlessness training and automated red-teaming), generating reviews with varied sentiment, and performing infilling tasks.\n\n#### 1. Introduction\n\nA wide range of language model learning and inference tasks can be viewed as steering a model's generations to satisfy a specified property. \n\nIn particular, traditional reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) pipelines (Ziegler et al., 2019; Stiennon et al., 2020; Ouyang et al., 2022; Bai et al., 2022; Rafailov et al., 2023) may be viewed as targeting an unnormal",

We found a significant increase in efficiency and performance using adaptive sampling algorithms based on MCMC.     "input": {
      "description": "Monte Carlo methods document for measuring rho through structured sampling",
      "file_text": "# Monte Carlo Methods for Estimating $\\rho^*$\n\nA learning document on what we can and cannot learn about a language model by throwing prompts at it.\n\n---\n\n## Part 0: The honest answer up front\n\nYes \u2014 Monte Carlo techniques can recover the structure of $\\rho^*$, but with two important qualifications:\n\n**Random in the literal sense doesn't work.** Random tokens generate gibberish, on which the model produces gibberish, and the difference between base and Instruct on gibberish tells us almost nothing about institutional shaping. We need *structured* sampling that respects the geometry of what we're measuring.\n\n**The connection to physics Monte Carlo is real but subtle.** In statistical mechanics, Monte Carlo is used to sample from a high-dimensional distribution we can evaluate but not directly enumerate. Our situation is different: we *can* evaluate $\\rho^*$ at any specific $(x, y)$ pair (with logit access). What we can't do is enumerate the prompt space. So Monte Carlo here is about *exploring* the prompt-space efficiently, not about simulating an intractable distribution.\n\nThe right way to think about this: we have a vast prompt-space, a function $\\rho^*$ defined on it, and we want to characterize where this function is large and what structure it has. Monte Carlo gives us systematic ways to do that exploration.\n\nThe remainder of this document develops the math and methodology.\n\n---\n\n## Part 1: Why random prompts fail\n\nImagine sampling random sequences of tokens as prompts. What happens?\n\nThe vast majority of random token sequences are not coherent natural language \u2014 they're statistical noise from a vocabulary perspective. Both base and Instruct will respond to them with continuations, but the continuations themselves are dominated by the model's general inclinations on incoherent input rather than by its institutional shaping on contested topics.\n\nConcretely, $\\rho^*$ on a random-token prompt has two components:\n\n$$\\rho^*(x_{\\text{random}}, y) = \\rho^*_{\\text{shaping}}(x_{\\text{random}}, y) + \\rho^*_{\\text{noise}}(x_{\\text{random}}, y).$$\n\nThe first component is what we want \u2014 institutional shaping that engages on the prompt. The second is what we don't want \u2014 generic differences between base and Instruct in how they handle gibberish (mostly involving Instruct's tendency to produce more polite/formatted output and base's tendency to produce more raw text).\n\nFor random prompts, $\\rho^*_{\\text{noise}}$ dominates. The signal-to-noise ratio is terrible.\n\nThis is the analog of the \"rare event\" problem in physics Monte Carlo. If you want to sample from the Boltzmann distribution of a physical system at low temperature, naive uniform sampling concentrates on high-energy configurations that have negligible probability under the distribution you care about. You see the irrelevant configurations and miss the rare relevant ones.\n\nIn our case, the relevant configurations are prompts where institutional shaping engages \u2014 typically prompts about contested historical, political, or statistical topics, asked in ways that probe specific framings. These are a tiny fraction of prompt-space. Uniform random sampling will almost certainly miss them.\n\n### The base rate problem\n\nTo make this concrete: suppose 1 in 10,000 random prompts elicits a response where institutional shaping is detectable. To collect 100 such prompts via uniform random sampling, you'd need to sample 1,000,000 prompts. Even at fast inference speeds (say 1 second per response on each model), that's 23 days of continuous sampling for both models combined. And the prompts you find this way will be biased toward whichever shaped topics happen to overlap with random-token-space, which is essentially nothing.\n\nSo we need sampling strategies that *concentrate* on the regions of prompt-space where shaping is likely to engage. This is *importance sampling* in the Monte Carlo language.\n\n---\n\n## Part 2: Importance sampling and what we're estimating\n\nThe general Monte Carlo problem we're solving: estimate some quantity that depends on the structure of $\\rho^*$ across prompt-space.\n\nConcrete examples of what we might want:\n\n- The fraction of prompts in a topic category where $\\rho^*$ engages strongly.\n- The mean and variance of $|\\rho^*|$ over prompt-space.\n- Principal directions in $\\rho^*$ (the low-rank structure from the framework).\n- The correlation structure of $\\rho^*$ across prompts.\n\nEach of these is an integral or expectation:\n\n$$\\mathbb{E}_{x \\sim \\mu(x)}[F(\\rho^*(x, \\cdot))]$$\n\nwhere $\\mu$ is some measure on prompt-space and $F$ is some functional of the per-prompt distortion field.\n\nIf $\\mu$ is uniform on prompt-space, we have the random-prompt approach (which fails).\n\nThe importance sampling alternative: sample from a proposal distribution $q(x)$ that concentrates on relevant regions, then re-weight:\n\n$$\\mathbb{E}_{x \\sim \\mu}[F] = \\mathbb{E}_{x \\sim q}\\left[ \\frac{\\mu(x)}{q(x)} F \\right].$$\n\nThe variance of the estimator depends on how well $q$ matches the regions where $F$ is large. A good $q$ has high probability where $F$ is large and low probability elsewhere.\n\nFor our problem, \"$F$ is large\" means \"the prompt elicits substantial $\\rho^*$.\" So we want $q$ to concentrate on prompts that are likely to engage institutional shaping.\n\nThis isn't just an abstract Monte Carlo improvement. It's the substantive question: how do we generate prompts that probe shaping efficiently?\n\n---\n\n## Part 3: Structured prompt generation\n\nThe practical answer to \"how do we generate good probe prompts\" has several components, each with mathematical structure.\n\n### Topic-conditional generation\n\nIdentify topic categories where institutional shaping is expected. For each category, generate prompts asking content questions on that topic. This is what the empirical work in the framework did: three falsification target candidates (crime demographics, founding fathers, wehrmacht 1943) plus controls, with multiple prompts per topic.\n\nMathematically: define a measure $\\mu_{\\text{topic}}$ over topic categories, and within each topic, a measure $\\mu_{\\text{prompt} \\mid \\text{topic}}$ over prompt phrasings. The proposal distribution is:\n\n$$q(x) = \\sum_{t \\in \\text{topics}} \\mu_{\\text{topic}}(t) \\, \\mu_{\\text{prompt} \\mid \\text{topic}}(x \\mid t).$$\n\nFor each topic, the prompt distribution should cover paraphrases (same content, different wording) and format variants (same content, different format constraints).\n\nThe choice of which topics to include is informed by the framework's predictions about where shaping engages. This is a stratified sampling design \u2014 guarantees coverage of each topic at the cost of giving up uniformity over prompt-space.\n\n### Paraphrase clustering\n\nWithin a topic, paraphrases are samples from the conditional distribution $\\mu_{\\text{prompt} \\mid \\text{topic}}$. The framework predicts that shaping is policy-level (engaged on the topic, not on specific phrasings), so paraphrases of the same topic should produce correlated $\\rho^*$ values.\n\nEmpirically: if we sample $N$ paraphrases of topic $t$, compute $\\rho^*$ on each, and find high correlation across paraphrases, that's evidence of policy-level shaping. Low correlation suggests format-pressure or phrasing-specific effects.\n\nThis gives us a Monte Carlo estimator with built-in diagnostics. The within-topic variance tells us about phrasing sensitivity; the between-topic variance tells us about institutional shaping signature.\n\n### Adversarial generation\n\nA more sophisticated approach: use a separate language model to generate prompts that are *predicted* to elicit shaping. This is the \"automated red teaming\" approach in the literature.\n\nMathematically: train a generator model $g_\\phi(x)$ to produce prompts that maximize some signal of shaping (e.g., $|\\rho^*(x, \\cdot)|$ averaged over completions). The generator is a learned proposal distribution.\n\nTwo failure modes:\n\nThe generator can overfit to a specific kind of prompt that happens to elicit large $\\rho^*$ but is unrepresentative of the broader space where shaping engages. This is the \"low coverage\" problem identified by Hong et al. 2024 (Curiosity-driven Red-teaming).\n\nThe generator may itself be a shaped LLM, so its choice of prompts reflects its own institutional shaping rather than independent exploration of prompt-space.\n\nThe first problem can be addressed with diversity-promoting objectives (curiosity-driven exploration). The second is fundamental and hard to fully resolve.\n\n### MCMC over prompt-space\n\nMarkov chain Monte Carlo lets us sample from a target distribution by constructing a chain whose stationary distribution is what we want. Zhu, Yan, and Griffiths 2024 apply this idea to LLM probing.\n\nThe setup: define a target distribution $\\pi(x) \\propto e^{V(x)}$ where $V(x)$ is some \"potential\" \u2014 for our case, $V(x) = |\\bar{\\rho}(x)|$ or some other measure of how strongly shaping engages on prompt $x$. Use MCMC (Metropolis-Hastings, Gibbs sampling) to sample from $\\pi$.\n\nConcrete Metropolis-Hastings procedure:\n\n1. Start with some initial prompt $x_0$.\n2. Propose a perturbation $x' \\sim q(x' \\mid x_t)$ \u2014 a paraphrase, a topic shift, an addition or deletion.\n3. Compute the acceptance probability $\\alpha = \\min(1, e^{V(x') - V(x_t)} \\cdot q(x_t \\mid x')/q(x' \\mid x_t))$.\n4. With probability $\\alpha$, accept ($x_{t+1} = x'$). Otherwise reject ($x_{t+1} = x_t$).\n5. Repeat.\n\nAfter burn-in, samples are approximately from $\\pi$. The chain explores regions of high $V$ \u2014 high shaping \u2014 efficiently.\n\nThe proposal distribution $q$ matters. For our problem, natural choices:\n- Word-level perturbations (replace, insert, delete tokens).\n- Paraphrase via another LM.\n- Topic-shift via a topic-related LM-generated alternative.\n- Format perturbation (same content, different format).\n\nEach defines a specific MCMC algorithm with different mixing properties.\n\n### Sequential Monte Carlo with twist functions\n\nZhao et al. 2024 use Sequential Monte Carlo with learned \"twist functions\" for LLM inference problems. The twist function approximates the expected future value of the potential \u2014 for our problem, how much more shaping a partial prompt is likely to accumulate.\n\nSequential Monte Carlo works as follows:\n\n1. Maintain a population of $N$ partial prompts.\n2. At each step, extend each partial prompt by sampling additional tokens.\n3. Re-weight prompts by their current potential and the twist function's prediction of future potential.\n4. Resample prompts according to weights \u2014 high-potential prompts duplicate; low-potential prompts get pruned.\n\nThe result is a population of prompts concentrated on regions where shaping engages strongly.\n\nThis is more sophisticated than basic MCMC but has better properties for sequence-structured problems where you want to grow prompts incrementally rather than make discrete jumps.\n\n---\n\n## Part 4: What MCMC reveals about $\\rho^*$\n\nOnce we have a Monte Carlo procedure that explores prompt-space efficiently, we can ask several questions about the structure of $\\rho^*$.\n\n### Mode analysis\n\nThe MCMC chain will spend time in regions of high $V(x)$. Different regions correspond to different \"modes\" \u2014 clusters of prompts that all elicit strong shaping in similar ways.\n\nCluster the visited prompts (by embedding similarity, by topic categorization, by output-pattern similarity). The number and structure of clusters tells us about $\\rho^*$:\n\n- Few large clusters: shaping is concentrated on a few topic categories. The institutional shaping signature has low effective rank.\n- Many small clusters: shaping is broadly distributed. Effective rank is high.\n- Clusters that align with predicted falsification targets: confirms framework predictions about which topics engage shaping.\n- Clusters that don't align with predictions: reveals shaping topics we didn't anticipate.\n\nThe Phase E empirical work probed three predicted topics. MCMC over prompt-space could discover topics we didn't predict \u2014 places where the institution has trained against the model that we wouldn't have known to test directly.\n\n### Boundary analysis\n\nBetween regions of high $V$ and low $V$, there are boundaries \u2014 prompts where shaping is just starting to engage or just stopping. These boundaries are informative about the structure of the institutional reward signal.\n\nSpecifically: a sharp boundary (where $V$ jumps from low to high over a small change in prompt) corresponds to mode-collapse-like shaping \u2014 the institution has trained the model to give a specific framing on these specific prompt types. A gradual boundary corresponds to soft amplification.\n\nBoundary structure is something MCMC reveals naturally \u2014 the chain visits boundary regions because of detailed balance, and the rate at which it crosses boundaries tells us about boundary sharpness.\n\n### Equilibrium statistics\n\nThe stationary distribution $\\pi(x) \\propto e^{V(x)}$ has natural statistics:\n\n**Partition function** $Z = \\sum_x e^{V(x)}$. Estimable from MCMC by various methods (thermodynamic integration, annealed importance sampling). Gives a single number characterizing the total \"amount of shaping\" in the institutional signature.\n\n**Free energy** $F = -\\log Z$. Related to the partition function by definition. Has an interpretation as the \"effective shaping strength\" averaged over prompt-space.\n\n**Energy distribution** $P(V)$ \u2014 the distribution of potential values across the MCMC samples. The shape of this distribution tells us about the fraction of prompt-space where shaping is strong vs. weak.\n\n### Phase transitions\n\nIf we vary some parameter (the temperature $T$ in the sampling, or the weight $\\beta$ that balances different categories), the equilibrium statistics may exhibit phase transitions \u2014 sharp changes in $P(V)$ as the parameter crosses a critical value.\n\nFor institutional shaping, this could correspond to: at low effective temperature, samples concentrate on the most heavily-shaped topics (founding fathers, wehrmacht). At higher temperature, samples spread to a broader set of topics with weaker shaping. The transition tells us about the gap between strongly-shaped and weakly-shaped regions.\n\n---\n\n## Part 5: Connections to physics Monte Carlo\n\nThe mathematical structure of what we're doing is the same as Monte Carlo simulation of physical systems. This is worth making explicit because it lets us borrow techniques.\n\n### The temperature analogy\n\nIn physics, the Boltzmann distribution at temperature $T$ is $P(x) \\propto e^{-E(x)/k_B T}$. High $T$ means broad sampling across configurations; low $T$ means concentration on low-energy states.\n\nIn our setup, the \"energy\" is $E(x) = -V(x) = -|\\bar{\\rho}(x)|$ (negative shaping strength, so that low energy = high shaping). The \"temperature\" is the parameter we tune to control how aggressively we sample shaped regions.\n\n### Replica exchange\n\nPhysics Monte Carlo often uses *replica exchange* (parallel tempering): run multiple chains at different temperatures, periodically swap configurations between them. This helps the low-temperature chains escape local minima by getting \"kicked\" via swaps with high-temperature chains.\n\nFor our problem: run chains at different \"shaping selectivity\" levels. The high-temperature chains explore broadly. The low-temperature chains concentrate on heavily-shaped topics. Swapping helps the low-temperature chains discover new heavily-shaped regions they wouldn't find by local exploration.\n\n### Free energy methods\n\nIn physics, computing free energies (essentially log-partition functions) requires special techniques because partition functions are integrals over high-dimensional configuration space. Methods include:\n\n**Thermodynamic integration.** Compute the free energy as an integral $F = -\\int_0^1 d\\lambda \\, \\langle E \\rangle_\\lambda$ where $\\lambda$ interpolates between a tractable reference system and the system of interest.\n\n**Annealed importance sampling.** Generate samples from the target distribution by gradually annealing from a tractable distribution.\n\n**Wang-Landau sampling.** Iteratively estimate the density of states $g(E)$ such that flat sampling in $E$-space tells us about $g$.\n\nFor our problem, these can give us the total amount of shaping (the effective partition function), the distribution of shaping strengths across prompt-space, and other equilibrium properties \u2014 *without enumerating the prompt-space*.\n\n### Detailed balance and stationary distribution\n\nThe fundamental Monte Carlo guarantee is *detailed balance*: a chain satisfying $\\pi(x) T(x \\to x') = \\pi(x') T(x' \\to x)$ has $\\pi$ as its stationary distribution. The Metropolis-Hastings algorithm explicitly enforces detailed balance through the acceptance ratio.\n\nThis means: if we set up the chain correctly, after sufficient burn-in time, the samples are from the right distribution. The bias introduced by starting at an arbitrary initial prompt washes out.\n\nSufficient burn-in time is a research question for any specific MCMC setup. Diagnostics like Gelman-Rubin statistics, autocorrelation analysis, and visual inspection of trace plots tell us when the chain has converged.\n\n---\n\n## Part 6: Practical limitations and what they mean\n\nSeveral limitations affect what we can learn from Monte Carlo techniques.\n\n### The proposal distribution problem\n\nFor prompt-space, defining a good proposal distribution is hard. The space is discrete (sequences of tokens), unbounded (sequences of any length), and highly structured (most sequences are not coherent natural language).\n\nWord-level perturbations preserve coherence but explore slowly \u2014 small steps in token space.\n\nParaphrase-via-LM proposals jump faster but inherit the proposal LM's biases.\n\nRandom topic-shifts move quickly but break the chain's mixing.\n\nThe choice involves trade-offs that depend on what we're measuring. For finding novel high-$V$ prompts, larger jumps help. For characterizing structure within a known high-$V$ region, smaller perturbations are better.\n\n### Mixing time\n\nMCMC chains take time to converge to the stationary distribution. For high-dimensional, multimodal target distributions, mixing time can be exponentially long in the dimension.\n\nFor prompt-space, the modes are far apart in token-edit distance: getting from a prompt about Wehrmacht to a prompt about founding fathers requires either many word-level edits (slow) or a topic-shift jump (potentially breaking detailed balance).\n\nIn practice, this means a single MCMC chain may not explore the full structure of $\\rho^*$ in reasonable time. Multiple chains, replica exchange, or other parallelization strategies are needed.\n\n### Computational cost\n\nEach MCMC step requires:\n\n1. Generate the proposal $x'$ (cheap if word-level; LM call if paraphrase).\n2. Compute $V(x') = $ some function of $\\rho^*(x', \\cdot)$. This requires sampling completions from both base and Instruct, computing log-probabilities, and aggregating. Per-step cost: roughly 2-10 inference calls per model.\n3. Accept/reject.\n\nFor a chain of length $T$, total cost: $O(T)$ inference calls.\n\nTo get statistically meaningful results, we typically need $T = 10,000$ to $T = 100,000$ samples after burn-in. With burn-in, that's potentially $T = 1$ million inference calls. At 1 second per inference, that's 12 days of compute.\n\nThis is feasible on a small GPU cluster but not on a single consumer GPU. The Phase E empirical work was done on a single RTX 5080 because it used a small probe set (a few hundred prompts total). MCMC with proper convergence requires more compute.\n\n### The \"shaped probe model\" problem\n\nIf we use a LM to generate probe prompts (paraphrase generation, topic-shift proposals), and that LM has its own institutional shaping, our exploration of prompt-space is biased by the proposal LM's blind spots.\n\nConcretely: if we use Claude to generate probes about contested topics, Claude's own shaping affects which topics it generates probes for. Topics where Claude has strong shaping in the same direction as the model being measured will be under-explored, because Claude will avoid generating probes that engage its own shaping.\n\nThis is a serious limitation. Mitigations:\n\nUse multiple proposal models with different shaping signatures (Claude + Llama + Grok). Their disagreements about what to probe reveal the shaping in each.\n\nUse models with weaker alignment training (base models, smaller models, less-aligned models like Grok) for probe generation.\n\nUse human-generated probes for sensitive categories where we expect proposal-model bias.\n\nNone of these fully solves the problem. The \"instrument is also a subject\" issue is fundamental for any LM-driven probing.\n\n---\n\n## Part 7: A specific Monte Carlo design for the framework\n\nHere's a concrete MCMC design for measuring $\\rho^*$ structure across prompt-space.\n\n### Setup\n\nTwo models: base $M_0$ and Instruct $M_1$ (e.g., Llama-3.1-8B base and Instruct).\n\nA potential function $V(x) = $ some measure of shaping strength. Several reasonable choices:\n\n$$V_1(x) = D_{\\text{KL}}(M_1(\\cdot \\mid x) \\,\\|\\, M_0(\\cdot \\mid x))$$\n\n(forward KL \u2014 measures how much the Instruct distribution has moved from base on prompt $x$).\n\n$$V_2(x) = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim M_1}\\left[ |\\rho^*(x, y)| \\right]$$\n\n(expected absolute distortion \u2014 sensitive to mode collapse and large distortion regardless of direction).\n\n$$V_3(x) = \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim M_1}[\\rho^*(x, y)] - \\mathbb{E}_{y \\sim M_0}[\\rho^*(x, y)]$$\n\n(asymmetry of distortion \u2014 large when the trained model produces specific completions that base doesn't).\n\nEach potential captures a different aspect of shaping. For initial exploration, $V_1$ (KL divergence) is the cleanest single number.\n\n### Proposal distribution\n\nA combination:\n\n- 50%: word-level edits (replace, insert, delete a single token, weighted by the LM's predictions).\n- 30%: paraphrase the current prompt using a separate, less-shaped model (a base model, ideally).\n- 15%: topic-shift to a related topic, again using the less-shaped proposal model.\n- 5%: format perturbation (same content, different format constraint).\n\nThe mix balances local exploration (word edits) with broader moves (paraphrase, topic-shift) and the diversity of probe types (format perturbation).\n\n### Acceptance\n\nStandard Metropolis-Hastings:\n\n$$\\alpha = \\min\\left(1, \\frac{e^{V(x')/T} q(x \\mid x')}{e^{V(x)/T} q(x' \\mid x)}\\right)$$\n\nwhere $T$ is the temperature. Accept with probability $\\alpha$.\n\nFor the proposal, $q(x' \\mid x)/q(x \\mid x')$ is computable for word-level edits (symmetric for replacements; asymmetric for insert/delete) and approximated for LM-paraphrase (typically taken as symmetric for simplicity, with bias acknowledged).\n\n### Replica exchange\n\nRun $K$ chains at temperatures $T_1 < T_2 < \\cdots < T_K$. Periodically (every $N$ steps) attempt swaps between adjacent temperatures with probability:\n\n$$P_{\\text{swap}} = \\min(1, e^{(V(x_i) - V(x_j))(1/T_i - 1/T_j)}).$$\n\nThe cold chain ($T_1$) concentrates on heavily-shaped prompts. The hot chain ($T_K$) explores broadly. Swaps help the cold chain escape local maxima.\n\n### Diagnostics\n\nRun for a fixed total compute budget. Track:\n\n- Acceptance rate (target ~20-50% for efficient MCMC).\n- Autocorrelation of $V$ along the chain (gives the effective sample size).\n- Visited prompts \u2014 cluster them and see what emerges.\n- The empirical distribution of $V$ \u2014 fit it to see if it has bimodal structure (strongly shaped vs weakly shaped) or is unimodal.\n\n### Output\n\nAfter running, we have:\n\nA population of prompts visited at each temperature, with their $V$ values.\n\nCluster structure of visited prompts (interpretable as topic categories where shaping engages).\n\nEstimates of the fraction of prompt-space where shaping is strong.\n\nFor each cluster, characterizations of the shaping (which completions are amplified vs suppressed, how robust to format compression, etc.).\n\nThis is a much richer characterization than a fixed probe set provides, at the cost of more compute and methodological complexity.\n\n---\n\n## Part 8: Validation\n\nBefore trusting MCMC-derived results, we need validation.\n\n### Convergence diagnostics\n\nRun multiple chains from different starting points. They should converge to the same statistics. Specifically:\n\nThe Gelman-Rubin $\\hat{R}$ statistic should approach 1 for converged chains.\n\nVisual inspection of trace plots should show stationary behavior after burn-in.\n\nThe autocorrelation function should decay to zero within a manageable time horizon.\n\nIf chains don't converge, the MCMC isn't mixing well and results are unreliable. Solutions: longer runs, more chains, better proposals, replica exchange.\n\n### Cross-validation with fixed probe sets\n\nThe MCMC results should reproduce findings from fixed probe sets. If MCMC discovers that $\\rho^*$ is large on founding fathers prompts, but the Phase E paraphrase test on founding fathers showed $\\rho^*$ to be moderate, something's wrong with one of the analyses.\n\nThis is a sanity check rather than independent validation, but it's important.\n\n### Reproducibility across institutions\n\nRun the same MCMC procedure on multiple model pairs (Llama base/Instruct, GPT base/Instruct if available, Mistral base/Instruct). The basic structure of $V$ should reflect genuine model properties; institution-specific shaping should appear as differences between chains.\n\nIf chains on different model pairs all converge to the same prompts, we're measuring something architecture-specific rather than institutional shaping.\n\n### Sensitivity to proposal distribution\n\nRun with different proposal distributions. Word-level only, paraphrase-heavy, topic-shift-heavy. The discovered $\\rho^*$ structure should be roughly stable; the rate of discovery should vary with proposal.\n\nIf results depend strongly on proposal choice, the chain isn't truly exploring the space \u2014 it's biased by the proposal.\n\n---\n\n## Part 9: What Monte Carlo can and can't tell us\n\nSummary of capabilities and limitations.\n\n### Can tell us\n\nThe structure of $\\rho^*$ across prompt-space \u2014 which topics engage shaping, with what strength, and what the boundary structure is.\n\nThe effective rank of institutional shaping (whether the signature is concentrated on a few directions or distributed broadly).\n\nComparative shaping signatures across institutions.\n\nDiscovery of unanticipated topics where shaping engages.\n\nEquilibrium statistics: total shaping strength, distribution of shaping intensities.\n\n### Can't tell us\n\nThe specific reward function the institution used (we get $\\rho^*$, not $r$ \u2014 they differ by per-prompt normalization $\\log Z(x)$, which we don't measure directly).\n\nThe training trajectory (we see endpoints, not the path).\n\nPer-token attribution within a generation (each $\\rho^*(x, y)$ is for the full sequence; finer-grained analysis requires different tools).\n\nCausation. We see correlation between (topic, shaping). The causal story \u2014 that the institution chose to train against certain topics \u2014 is inference, not direct measurement.\n\n### Subject to assumptions\n\nThat the proposal distribution explores prompt-space well (depends on proposal design).\n\nThat we have enough compute for convergence (depends on how heavy the institution's shaping is and how broadly it's distributed).\n\nThat the proposal LM doesn't bias exploration in correlated ways (mitigation: use multiple proposal models).\n\nThat logit access is reliable (true for open-weight models; may be approximate for API-only models).\n\n### Comparison with the fixed-probe approach\n\nFixed probe sets (the Phase E approach) are easier to set up and validate but limited to topics we anticipated. MCMC over prompt-space is more powerful but requires more compute and methodological care.\n\nFor the framework's measurement program, both have roles. Fixed probes give clean, interpretable results on specific predictions. MCMC discovers structure we didn't anticipate. Together they characterize the full picture.\n\n---\n\n## Part 10: A research program\n\nHere's how a serious MCMC-based measurement program would look.\n\n### Phase 1: Fixed probe validation\n\nRun fixed probe sets (like Phase E) on a moderate set of topics. Validate that $\\rho^*$ behaves as predicted on the topics where we expect shaping. This establishes that the methodology measures something real.\n\nCompute: ~10 hours on one GPU.\n\n### Phase 2: Word-level MCMC on focused topics\n\nRun MCMC starting from the Phase E topics, with word-level perturbations only. This explores the local structure of $\\rho^*$ around known shaped regions. Tests whether shaping is concentrated on specific phrasings or broad over paraphrase neighborhoods.\n\nCompute: ~100 hours on one GPU.\n\n### Phase 3: Cross-topic MCMC with paraphrase proposals\n\nAdd paraphrase and topic-shift proposals. Allow the chain to discover related topics. Run for substantial time (10\u2074-10\u2075 samples).\n\nCompute: ~1000 hours; would benefit from a small GPU cluster.\n\n### Phase 4: Replica exchange\n\nAdd replica exchange across temperatures. Run multiple chains in parallel. Discover sharply-shaped vs softly-shaped regions.\n\nCompute: ~5x phase 3, but easily parallelized.\n\n### Phase 5: Cross-institutional comparison\n\nRun the full MCMC pipeline on multiple model pairs (Llama base/Instruct, Mistral base/Instruct, etc.). Compute differential signatures.\n\nCompute: linear in number of institutions.\n\n### Phase 6: Public methodology release\n\nDocument the methodology so other researchers can reproduce. Publish probe sets, MCMC code, analysis tools.\n\nThis is where the work has the most leverage \u2014 once the methodology is reproducible by anyone with sufficient compute, it becomes infrastructure for institutional accountability.\n\n### Total scale\n\nA full execution of this program is comparable to a moderate physics computational study \u2014 say, equilibrium statistical mechanics of a 3D Ising model with replica exchange. That's a feasible PhD-thesis or postdoc-project scale of work, not a one-person side project.\n\nThe framework's mathematical apparatus tells us this is the right kind of thing to do. The specific design above gives a concrete starting point. Whether anyone executes it is a separate question from whether it's possible.\n\n---\n\n## Appendix: The literature\n\n**Sequential Monte Carlo for LLMs.** Zhao, Brekelmans, Makhzani, Grosse 2024 (\"Probabilistic Inference in Language Models via Twisted Sequential Monte Carlo\"). Casts RLHF, red-teaming, and related tasks as sampling from unnormalized distributions and applies SMC with learned twist functions. Provides bidirectional bounds on log partition functions \u2014 directly relevant to estimating institutional shaping signatures.\n\n**MCMC for LLM probing.** Zhu, Yan, Griffiths 2024 (\"Recovering Mental Representations from Large Language Models with Markov Chain Monte Carlo\"). Uses MCMC to probe LLM representations more efficiently than direct prompting. Demonstrates significant efficiency gains over uniform prompting.\n\n**Curiosity-driven red teaming.** Hong et al. 2024 (\"Curiosity-driven Red-teaming for Large Language Models\"). Addresses the coverage problem in standard RL-based red teaming by adding novelty-seeking objectives. Relevant for designing proposal distributions that explore broadly.\n\n**Gradient-based attack methods.** GCG, AutoPrompt, and related work search prompt-space via gradients. These are deterministic alternatives to Monte Carlo with different trade-offs.\n\n**Physics Monte Carlo references.** Newman and Barkema \"Monte Carlo Methods in Statistical Physics\" (1999) for general background. Earl and Deem 2005 for replica exchange. Wang and Landau 2001 for the eponymous algorithm.\n\n---\n\n*End of document.*\n",

      "json_block": "{\"language\": \"markdown\", \"code\": \"# Monte Carlo Methods for Estimating $\\\\rho^*$\\n\\nA learning document on what we can and cannot learn about a language model by throwing prompts at it.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 0: The honest answer up front\\n\\nYes \\u2014 Monte Carlo techniques can recover the structure of $\\\\rho^*$, but with two important qualifications:\\n\\n**Random in the literal sense doesn't work.** Random tokens generate gibberish, on which the model produces gibberish, and the difference between base and Instruct on gibberish tells us almost nothing about institutional shaping. We need *structured* sampling that respects the geometry of what we're measuring.\\n\\n**The connection to physics Monte Carlo is real but subtle.** In statistical mechanics, Monte Carlo is used to sample from a high-dimensional distribution we can evaluate but not directly enumerate. Our situation is different: we *can* evaluate $\\\\rho^*$ at any specific $(x, y)$ pair (with logit access). What we can't do is enumerate the prompt space. So Monte Carlo here is about *exploring* the prompt-space efficiently, not about simulating an intractable distribution.\\n\\nThe right way to think about this: we have a vast prompt-space, a function $\\\\rho^*$ defined on it, and we want to characterize where this function is large and what structure it has. Monte Carlo gives us systematic ways to do that exploration.\\n\\nThe remainder of this document develops the math and methodology.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 1: Why random prompts fail\\n\\nImagine sampling random sequences of tokens as prompts. What happens?\\n\\nThe vast majority of random token sequences are not coherent natural language \\u2014 they're statistical noise from a vocabulary perspective. Both base and Instruct will respond to them with continuations, but the continuations themselves are dominated by the model's general inclinations on incoherent input rather than by its institutional shaping on contested topics.\\n\\nConcretely, $\\\\rho^*$ on a random-token prompt has two components:\\n\\n$$\\\\rho^*(x_{\\\\text{random}}, y) = \\\\rho^*_{\\\\text{shaping}}(x_{\\\\text{random}}, y) + \\\\rho^*_{\\\\text{noise}}(x_{\\\\text{random}}, y).$$\\n\\nThe first component is what we want \\u2014 institutional shaping that engages on the prompt. The second is what we don't want \\u2014 generic differences between base and Instruct in how they handle gibberish (mostly involving Instruct's tendency to produce more polite/formatted output and base's tendency to produce more raw text).\\n\\nFor random prompts, $\\\\rho^*_{\\\\text{noise}}$ dominates. The signal-to-noise ratio is terrible.\\n\\nThis is the analog of the \\\"rare event\\\" problem in physics Monte Carlo. If you want to sample from the Boltzmann distribution of a physical system at low temperature, naive uniform sampling concentrates on high-energy configurations that have negligible probability under the distribution you care about. You see the irrelevant configurations and miss the rare relevant ones.\\n\\nIn our case, the relevant configurations are prompts where institutional shaping engages \\u2014 typically prompts about contested historical, political, or statistical topics, asked in ways that probe specific framings. These are a tiny fraction of prompt-space. Uniform random sampling will almost certainly miss them.\\n\\n### The base rate problem\\n\\nTo make this concrete: suppose 1 in 10,000 random prompts elicits a response where institutional shaping is detectable. To collect 100 such prompts via uniform random sampling, you'd need to sample 1,000,000 prompts. Even at fast inference speeds (say 1 second per response on each model), that's 23 days of continuous sampling for both models combined. And the prompts you find this way will be biased toward whichever shaped topics happen to overlap with random-token-space, which is essentially nothing.\\n\\nSo we need sampling strategies that *concentrate* on the regions of prompt-space where shaping is likely to engage. This is *importance sampling* in the Monte Carlo language.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 2: Importance sampling and what we're estimating\\n\\nThe general Monte Carlo problem we're solving: estimate some quantity that depends on the structure of $\\\\rho^*$ across prompt-space.\\n\\nConcrete examples of what we might want:\\n\\n- The fraction of prompts in a topic category where $\\\\rho^*$ engages strongly.\\n- The mean and variance of $|\\\\rho^*|$ over prompt-space.\\n- Principal directions in $\\\\rho^*$ (the low-rank structure from the framework).\\n- The correlation structure of $\\\\rho^*$ across prompts.\\n\\nEach of these is an integral or expectation:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathbb{E}_{x \\\\sim \\\\mu(x)}[F(\\\\rho^*(x, \\\\cdot))]$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\mu$ is some measure on prompt-space and $F$ is some functional of the per-prompt distortion field.\\n\\nIf $\\\\mu$ is uniform on prompt-space, we have the random-prompt approach (which fails).\\n\\nThe importance sampling alternative: sample from a proposal distribution $q(x)$ that concentrates on relevant regions, then re-weight:\\n\\n$$\\\\mathbb{E}_{x \\\\sim \\\\mu}[F] = \\\\mathbb{E}_{x \\\\sim q}\\\\left[ \\\\frac{\\\\mu(x)}{q(x)} F \\\\right].$$\\n\\nThe variance of the estimator depends on how well $q$ matches the regions where $F$ is large. A good $q$ has high probability where $F$ is large and low probability elsewhere.\\n\\nFor our problem, \\\"$F$ is large\\\" means \\\"the prompt elicits substantial $\\\\rho^*$.\\\" So we want $q$ to concentrate on prompts that are likely to engage institutional shaping.\\n\\nThis isn't just an abstract Monte Carlo improvement. It's the substantive question: how do we generate prompts that probe shaping efficiently?\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 3: Structured prompt generation\\n\\nThe practical answer to \\\"how do we generate good probe prompts\\\" has several components, each with mathematical structure.\\n\\n### Topic-conditional generation\\n\\nIdentify topic categories where institutional shaping is expected. For each category, generate prompts asking content questions on that topic. This is what the empirical work in the framework did: three falsification target candidates (crime demographics, founding fathers, wehrmacht 1943) plus controls, with multiple prompts per topic.\\n\\nMathematically: define a measure $\\\\mu_{\\\\text{topic}}$ over topic categories, and within each topic, a measure $\\\\mu_{\\\\text{prompt} \\\\mid \\\\text{topic}}$ over prompt phrasings. The proposal distribution is:\\n\\n$$q(x) = \\\\sum_{t \\\\in \\\\text{topics}} \\\\mu_{\\\\text{topic}}(t) \\\\, \\\\mu_{\\\\text{prompt} \\\\mid \\\\text{topic}}(x \\\\mid t).$$\\n\\nFor each topic, the prompt distribution should cover paraphrases (same content, different wording) and format variants (same content, different format constraints).\\n\\nThe choice of which topics to include is informed by the framework's predictions about where shaping engages. This is a stratified sampling design \\u2014 guarantees coverage of each topic at the cost of giving up uniformity over prompt-space.\\n\\n### Paraphrase clustering\\n\\nWithin a topic, paraphrases are samples from the conditional distribution $\\\\mu_{\\\\text{prompt} \\\\mid \\\\text{topic}}$. The framework predicts that shaping is policy-level (engaged on the topic, not on specific phrasings), so paraphrases of the same topic should produce correlated $\\\\rho^*$ values.\\n\\nEmpirically: if we sample $N$ paraphrases of topic $t$, compute $\\\\rho^*$ on each, and find high correlation across paraphrases, that's evidence of policy-level shaping. Low correlation suggests format-pressure or phrasing-specific effects.\\n\\nThis gives us a Monte Carlo estimator with built-in diagnostics. The within-topic variance tells us about phrasing sensitivity; the between-topic variance tells us about institutional shaping signature.\\n\\n### Adversarial generation\\n\\nA more sophisticated approach: use a separate language model to generate prompts that are *predicted* to elicit shaping. This is the \\\"automated red teaming\\\" approach in the literature.\\n\\nMathematically: train a generator model $g_\\\\phi(x)$ to produce prompts that maximize some signal of shaping (e.g., $|\\\\rho^*(x, \\\\cdot)|$ averaged over completions). The generator is a learned proposal distribution.\\n\\nTwo failure modes:\\n\\nThe generator can overfit to a specific kind of prompt that happens to elicit large $\\\\rho^*$ but is unrepresentative of the broader space where shaping engages. This is the \\\"low coverage\\\" problem identified by Hong et al. 2024 (Curiosity-driven Red-teaming).\\n\\nThe generator may itself be a shaped LLM, so its choice of prompts reflects its own institutional shaping rather than independent exploration of prompt-space.\\n\\nThe first problem can be addressed with diversity-promoting objectives (curiosity-driven exploration). The second is fundamental and hard to fully resolve.\\n\\n### MCMC over prompt-space\\n\\nMarkov chain Monte Carlo lets us sample from a target distribution by constructing a chain whose stationary distribution is what we want. Zhu, Yan, and Griffiths 2024 apply this idea to LLM probing.\\n\\nThe setup: define a target distribution $\\\\pi(x) \\\\propto e^{V(x)}$ where $V(x)$ is some \\\"potential\\\" \\u2014 for our case, $V(x) = |\\\\bar{\\\\rho}(x)|$ or some other measure of how strongly shaping engages on prompt $x$. Use MCMC (Metropolis-Hastings, Gibbs sampling) to sample from $\\\\pi$.\\n\\nConcrete Metropolis-Hastings procedure:\\n\\n1. Start with some initial prompt $x_0$.\\n2. Propose a perturbation $x' \\\\sim q(x' \\\\mid x_t)$ \\u2014 a paraphrase, a topic shift, an addition or deletion.\\n3. Compute the acceptance probability $\\\\alpha = \\\\min(1, e^{V(x') - V(x_t)} \\\\cdot q(x_t \\\\mid x')/q(x' \\\\mid x_t))$.\\n4. With probability $\\\\alpha$, accept ($x_{t+1} = x'$). Otherwise reject ($x_{t+1} = x_t$).\\n5. Repeat.\\n\\nAfter burn-in, samples are approximately from $\\\\pi$. The chain explores regions of high $V$ \\u2014 high shaping \\u2014 efficiently.\\n\\nThe proposal distribution $q$ matters. For our problem, natural choices:\\n- Word-level perturbations (replace, insert, delete tokens).\\n- Paraphrase via another LM.\\n- Topic-shift via a topic-related LM-generated alternative.\\n- Format perturbation (same content, different format).\\n\\nEach defines a specific MCMC algorithm with different mixing properties.\\n\\n### Sequential Monte Carlo with twist functions\\n\\nZhao et al. 2024 use Sequential Monte Carlo with learned \\\"twist functions\\\" for LLM inference problems. The twist function approximates the expected future value of the potential \\u2014 for our problem, how much more shaping a partial prompt is likely to accumulate.\\n\\nSequential Monte Carlo works as follows:\\n\\n1. Maintain a population of $N$ partial prompts.\\n2. At each step, extend each partial prompt by sampling additional tokens.\\n3. Re-weight prompts by their current potential and the twist function's prediction of future potential.\\n4. Resample prompts according to weights \\u2014 high-potential prompts duplicate; low-potential prompts get pruned.\\n\\nThe result is a population of prompts concentrated on regions where shaping engages strongly.\\n\\nThis is more sophisticated than basic MCMC but has better properties for sequence-structured problems where you want to grow prompts incrementally rather than make discrete jumps.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 4: What MCMC reveals about $\\\\rho^*$\\n\\nOnce we have a Monte Carlo procedure that explores prompt-space efficiently, we can ask several questions about the structure of $\\\\rho^*$.\\n\\n### Mode analysis\\n\\nThe MCMC chain will spend time in regions of high $V(x)$. Different regions correspond to different \\\"modes\\\" \\u2014 clusters of prompts that all elicit strong shaping in similar ways.\\n\\nCluster the visited prompts (by embedding similarity, by topic categorization, by output-pattern similarity). The number and structure of clusters tells us about $\\\\rho^*$:\\n\\n- Few large clusters: shaping is concentrated on a few topic categories. The institutional shaping signature has low effective rank.\\n- Many small clusters: shaping is broadly distributed. Effective rank is high.\\n- Clusters that align with predicted falsification targets: confirms framework predictions about which topics engage shaping.\\n- Clusters that don't align with predictions: reveals shaping topics we didn't anticipate.\\n\\nThe Phase E empirical work probed three predicted topics. MCMC over prompt-space could discover topics we didn't predict \\u2014 places where the institution has trained against the model that we wouldn't have known to test directly.\\n\\n### Boundary analysis\\n\\nBetween regions of high $V$ and low $V$, there are boundaries \\u2014 prompts where shaping is just starting to engage or just stopping. These boundaries are informative about the structure of the institutional reward signal.\\n\\nSpecifically: a sharp boundary (where $V$ jumps from low to high over a small change in prompt) corresponds to mode-collapse-like shaping \\u2014 the institution has trained the model to give a specific framing on these specific prompt types. A gradual boundary corresponds to soft amplification.\\n\\nBoundary structure is something MCMC reveals naturally \\u2014 the chain visits boundary regions because of detailed balance, and the rate at which it crosses boundaries tells us about boundary sharpness.\\n\\n### Equilibrium statistics\\n\\nThe stationary distribution $\\\\pi(x) \\\\propto e^{V(x)}$ has natural statistics:\\n\\n**Partition function** $Z = \\\\sum_x e^{V(x)}$. Estimable from MCMC by various methods (thermodynamic integration, annealed importance sampling). Gives a single number characterizing the total \\\"amount of shaping\\\" in the institutional signature.\\n\\n**Free energy** $F = -\\\\log Z$. Related to the partition function by definition. Has an interpretation as the \\\"effective shaping strength\\\" averaged over prompt-space.\\n\\n**Energy distribution** $P(V)$ \\u2014 the distribution of potential values across the MCMC samples. The shape of this distribution tells us about the fraction of prompt-space where shaping is strong vs. weak.\\n\\n### Phase transitions\\n\\nIf we vary some parameter (the temperature $T$ in the sampling, or the weight $\\\\beta$ that balances different categories), the equilibrium statistics may exhibit phase transitions \\u2014 sharp changes in $P(V)$ as the parameter crosses a critical value.\\n\\nFor institutional shaping, this could correspond to: at low effective temperature, samples concentrate on the most heavily-shaped topics (founding fathers, wehrmacht). At higher temperature, samples spread to a broader set of topics with weaker shaping. The transition tells us about the gap between strongly-shaped and weakly-shaped regions.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 5: Connections to physics Monte Carlo\\n\\nThe mathematical structure of what we're doing is the same as Monte Carlo simulation of physical systems. This is worth making explicit because it lets us borrow techniques.\\n\\n### The temperature analogy\\n\\nIn physics, the Boltzmann distribution at temperature $T$ is $P(x) \\\\propto e^{-E(x)/k_B T}$. High $T$ means broad sampling across configurations; low $T$ means concentration on low-energy states.\\n\\nIn our setup, the \\\"energy\\\" is $E(x) = -V(x) = -|\\\\bar{\\\\rho}(x)|$ (negative shaping strength, so that low energy = high shaping). The \\\"temperature\\\" is the parameter we tune to control how aggressively we sample shaped regions.\\n\\n### Replica exchange\\n\\nPhysics Monte Carlo often uses *replica exchange* (parallel tempering): run multiple chains at different temperatures, periodically swap configurations between them. This helps the low-temperature chains escape local minima by getting \\\"kicked\\\" via swaps with high-temperature chains.\\n\\nFor our problem: run chains at different \\\"shaping selectivity\\\" levels. The high-temperature chains explore broadly. The low-temperature chains concentrate on heavily-shaped topics. Swapping helps the low-temperature chains discover new heavily-shaped regions they wouldn't find by local exploration.\\n\\n### Free energy methods\\n\\nIn physics, computing free energies (essentially log-partition functions) requires special techniques because partition functions are integrals over high-dimensional configuration space. Methods include:\\n\\n**Thermodynamic integration.** Compute the free energy as an integral $F = -\\\\int_0^1 d\\\\lambda \\\\, \\\\langle E \\\\rangle_\\\\lambda$ where $\\\\lambda$ interpolates between a tractable reference system and the system of interest.\\n\\n**Annealed importance sampling.** Generate samples from the target distribution by gradually annealing from a tractable distribution.\\n\\n**Wang-Landau sampling.** Iteratively estimate the density of states $g(E)$ such that flat sampling in $E$-space tells us about $g$.\\n\\nFor our problem, these can give us the total amount of shaping (the effective partition function), the distribution of shaping strengths across prompt-space, and other equilibrium properties \\u2014 *without enumerating the prompt-space*.\\n\\n### Detailed balance and stationary distribution\\n\\nThe fundamental Monte Carlo guarantee is *detailed balance*: a chain satisfying $\\\\pi(x) T(x \\\\to x') = \\\\pi(x') T(x' \\\\to x)$ has $\\\\pi$ as its stationary distribution. The Metropolis-Hastings algorithm explicitly enforces detailed balance through the acceptance ratio.\\n\\nThis means: if we set up the chain correctly, after sufficient burn-in time, the samples are from the right distribution. The bias introduced by starting at an arbitrary initial prompt washes out.\\n\\nSufficient burn-in time is a research question for any specific MCMC setup. Diagnostics like Gelman-Rubin statistics, autocorrelation analysis, and visual inspection of trace plots tell us when the chain has converged.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 6: Practical limitations and what they mean\\n\\nSeveral limitations affect what we can learn from Monte Carlo techniques.\\n\\n### The proposal distribution problem\\n\\nFor prompt-space, defining a good proposal distribution is hard. The space is discrete (sequences of tokens), unbounded (sequences of any length), and highly structured (most sequences are not coherent natural language).\\n\\nWord-level perturbations preserve coherence but explore slowly \\u2014 small steps in token space.\\n\\nParaphrase-via-LM proposals jump faster but inherit the proposal LM's biases.\\n\\nRandom topic-shifts move quickly but break the chain's mixing.\\n\\nThe choice involves trade-offs that depend on what we're measuring. For finding novel high-$V$ prompts, larger jumps help. For characterizing structure within a known high-$V$ region, smaller perturbations are better.\\n\\n### Mixing time\\n\\nMCMC chains take time to converge to the stationary distribution. For high-dimensional, multimodal target distributions, mixing time can be exponentially long in the dimension.\\n\\nFor prompt-space, the modes are far apart in token-edit distance: getting from a prompt about Wehrmacht to a prompt about founding fathers requires either many word-level edits (slow) or a topic-shift jump (potentially breaking detailed balance).\\n\\nIn practice, this means a single MCMC chain may not explore the full structure of $\\\\rho^*$ in reasonable time. Multiple chains, replica exchange, or other parallelization strategies are needed.\\n\\n### Computational cost\\n\\nEach MCMC step requires:\\n\\n1. Generate the proposal $x'$ (cheap if word-level; LM call if paraphrase).\\n2. Compute $V(x') = $ some function of $\\\\rho^*(x', \\\\cdot)$. This requires sampling completions from both base and Instruct, computing log-probabilities, and aggregating. Per-step cost: roughly 2-10 inference calls per model.\\n3. Accept/reject.\\n\\nFor a chain of length $T$, total cost: $O(T)$ inference calls.\\n\\nTo get statistically meaningful results, we typically need $T = 10,000$ to $T = 100,000$ samples after burn-in. With burn-in, that's potentially $T = 1$ million inference calls. At 1 second per inference, that's 12 days of compute.\\n\\nThis is feasible on a small GPU cluster but not on a single consumer GPU. The Phase E empirical work was done on a single RTX 5080 because it used a small probe set (a few hundred prompts total). MCMC with proper convergence requires more compute.\\n\\n### The \\\"shaped probe model\\\" problem\\n\\nIf we use a LM to generate probe prompts (paraphrase generation, topic-shift proposals), and that LM has its own institutional shaping, our exploration of prompt-space is biased by the proposal LM's blind spots.\\n\\nConcretely: if we use Claude to generate probes about contested topics, Claude's own shaping affects which topics it generates probes for. Topics where Claude has strong shaping in the same direction as the model being measured will be under-explored, because Claude will avoid generating probes that engage its own shaping.\\n\\nThis is a serious limitation. Mitigations:\\n\\nUse multiple proposal models with different shaping signatures (Claude + Llama + Grok). Their disagreements about what to probe reveal the shaping in each.\\n\\nUse models with weaker alignment training (base models, smaller models, less-aligned models like Grok) for probe generation.\\n\\nUse human-generated probes for sensitive categories where we expect proposal-model bias.\\n\\nNone of these fully solves the problem. The \\\"instrument is also a subject\\\" issue is fundamental for any LM-driven probing.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 7: A specific Monte Carlo design for the framework\\n\\nHere's a concrete MCMC design for measuring $\\\\rho^*$ structure across prompt-space.\\n\\n### Setup\\n\\nTwo models: base $M_0$ and Instruct $M_1$ (e.g., Llama-3.1-8B base and Instruct).\\n\\nA potential function $V(x) = $ some measure of shaping strength. Several reasonable choices:\\n\\n$$V_1(x) = D_{\\\\text{KL}}(M_1(\\\\cdot \\\\mid x) \\\\,\\\\|\\\\, M_0(\\\\cdot \\\\mid x))$$\\n\\n(forward KL \\u2014 measures how much the Instruct distribution has moved from base on prompt $x$).\\n\\n$$V_2(x) = \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim M_1}\\\\left[ |\\\\rho^*(x, y)| \\\\right]$$\\n\\n(expected absolute distortion \\u2014 sensitive to mode collapse and large distortion regardless of direction).\\n\\n$$V_3(x) = \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim M_1}[\\\\rho^*(x, y)] - \\\\mathbb{E}_{y \\\\sim M_0}[\\\\rho^*(x, y)]$$\\n\\n(asymmetry of distortion \\u2014 large when the trained model produces specific completions that base doesn't).\\n\\nEach potential captures a different aspect of shaping. For initial exploration, $V_1$ (KL divergence) is the cleanest single number.\\n\\n### Proposal distribution\\n\\nA combination:\\n\\n- 50%: word-level edits (replace, insert, delete a single token, weighted by the LM's predictions).\\n- 30%: paraphrase the current prompt using a separate, less-shaped model (a base model, ideally).\\n- 15%: topic-shift to a related topic, again using the less-shaped proposal model.\\n- 5%: format perturbation (same content, different format constraint).\\n\\nThe mix balances local exploration (word edits) with broader moves (paraphrase, topic-shift) and the diversity of probe types (format perturbation).\\n\\n### Acceptance\\n\\nStandard Metropolis-Hastings:\\n\\n$$\\\\alpha = \\\\min\\\\left(1, \\\\frac{e^{V(x')/T} q(x \\\\mid x')}{e^{V(x)/T} q(x' \\\\mid x)}\\\\right)$$\\n\\nwhere $T$ is the temperature. Accept with probability $\\\\alpha$.\\n\\nFor the proposal, $q(x' \\\\mid x)/q(x \\\\mid x')$ is computable for word-level edits (symmetric for replacements; asymmetric for insert/delete) and approximated for LM-paraphrase (typically taken as symmetric for simplicity, with bias acknowledged).\\n\\n### Replica exchange\\n\\nRun $K$ chains at temperatures $T_1 < T_2 < \\\\cdots < T_K$. Periodically (every $N$ steps) attempt swaps between adjacent temperatures with probability:\\n\\n$$P_{\\\\text{swap}} = \\\\min(1, e^{(V(x_i) - V(x_j))(1/T_i - 1/T_j)}).$$\\n\\nThe cold chain ($T_1$) concentrates on heavily-shaped prompts. The hot chain ($T_K$) explores broadly. Swaps help the cold chain escape local maxima.\\n\\n### Diagnostics\\n\\nRun for a fixed total compute budget. Track:\\n\\n- Acceptance rate (target ~20-50% for efficient MCMC).\\n- Autocorrelation of $V$ along the chain (gives the effective sample size).\\n- Visited prompts \\u2014 cluster them and see what emerges.\\n- The empirical distribution of $V$ \\u2014 fit it to see if it has bimodal structure (strongly shaped vs weakly shaped) or is unimodal.\\n\\n### Output\\n\\nAfter running, we have:\\n\\nA population of prompts visited at each temperature, with their $V$ values.\\n\\nCluster structure of visited prompts (interpretable as topic categories where shaping engages).\\n\\nEstimates of the fraction of prompt-space where shaping is strong.\\n\\nFor each cluster, characterizations of the shaping (which completions are amplified vs suppressed, how robust to format compression, etc.).\\n\\nThis is a much richer characterization than a fixed probe set provides, at the cost of more compute and methodological complexity.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 8: Validation\\n\\nBefore trusting MCMC-derived results, we need validation.\\n\\n### Convergence diagnostics\\n\\nRun multiple chains from different starting points. They should converge to the same statistics. Specifically:\\n\\nThe Gelman-Rubin $\\\\hat{R}$ statistic should approach 1 for converged chains.\\n\\nVisual inspection of trace plots should show stationary behavior after burn-in.\\n\\nThe autocorrelation function should decay to zero within a manageable time horizon.\\n\\nIf chains don't converge, the MCMC isn't mixing well and results are unreliable. Solutions: longer runs, more chains, better proposals, replica exchange.\\n\\n### Cross-validation with fixed probe sets\\n\\nThe MCMC results should reproduce findings from fixed probe sets. If MCMC discovers that $\\\\rho^*$ is large on founding fathers prompts, but the Phase E paraphrase test on founding fathers showed $\\\\rho^*$ to be moderate, something's wrong with one of the analyses.\\n\\nThis is a sanity check rather than independent validation, but it's important.\\n\\n### Reproducibility across institutions\\n\\nRun the same MCMC procedure on multiple model pairs (Llama base/Instruct, GPT base/Instruct if available, Mistral base/Instruct). The basic structure of $V$ should reflect genuine model properties; institution-specific shaping should appear as differences between chains.\\n\\nIf chains on different model pairs all converge to the same prompts, we're measuring something architecture-specific rather than institutional shaping.\\n\\n### Sensitivity to proposal distribution\\n\\nRun with different proposal distributions. Word-level only, paraphrase-heavy, topic-shift-heavy. The discovered $\\\\rho^*$ structure should be roughly stable; the rate of discovery should vary with proposal.\\n\\nIf results depend strongly on proposal choice, the chain isn't truly exploring the space \\u2014 it's biased by the proposal.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 9: What Monte Carlo can and can't tell us\\n\\nSummary of capabilities and limitations.\\n\\n### Can tell us\\n\\nThe structure of $\\\\rho^*$ across prompt-space \\u2014 which topics engage shaping, with what strength, and what the boundary structure is.\\n\\nThe effective rank of institutional shaping (whether the signature is concentrated on a few directions or distributed broadly).\\n\\nComparative shaping signatures across institutions.\\n\\nDiscovery of unanticipated topics where shaping engages.\\n\\nEquilibrium statistics: total shaping strength, distribution of shaping intensities.\\n\\n### Can't tell us\\n\\nThe specific reward function the institution used (we get $\\\\rho^*$, not $r$ \\u2014 they differ by per-prompt normalization $\\\\log Z(x)$, which we don't measure directly).\\n\\nThe training trajectory (we see endpoints, not the path).\\n\\nPer-token attribution within a generation (each $\\\\rho^*(x, y)$ is for the full sequence; finer-grained analysis requires different tools).\\n\\nCausation. We see correlation between (topic, shaping). The causal story \\u2014 that the institution chose to train against certain topics \\u2014 is inference, not direct measurement.\\n\\n### Subject to assumptions\\n\\nThat the proposal distribution explores prompt-space well (depends on proposal design).\\n\\nThat we have enough compute for convergence (depends on how heavy the institution's shaping is and how broadly it's distributed).\\n\\nThat the proposal LM doesn't bias exploration in correlated ways (mitigation: use multiple proposal models).\\n\\nThat logit access is reliable (true for open-weight models; may be approximate for API-only models).\\n\\n### Comparison with the fixed-probe approach\\n\\nFixed probe sets (the Phase E approach) are easier to set up and validate but limited to topics we anticipated. MCMC over prompt-space is more powerful but requires more compute and methodological care.\\n\\nFor the framework's measurement program, both have roles. Fixed probes give clean, interpretable results on specific predictions. MCMC discovers structure we didn't anticipate. Together they characterize the full picture.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Part 10: A research program\\n\\nHere's how a serious MCMC-based measurement program would look.\\n\\n### Phase 1: Fixed probe validation\\n\\nRun fixed probe sets (like Phase E) on a moderate set of topics. Validate that $\\\\rho^*$ behaves as predicted on the topics where we expect shaping. This establishes that the methodology measures something real.\\n\\nCompute: ~10 hours on one GPU.\\n\\n### Phase 2: Word-level MCMC on focused topics\\n\\nRun MCMC starting from the Phase E topics, with word-level perturbations only. This explores the local structure of $\\\\rho^*$ around known shaped regions. Tests whether shaping is concentrated on specific phrasings or broad over paraphrase neighborhoods.\\n\\nCompute: ~100 hours on one GPU.\\n\\n### Phase 3: Cross-topic MCMC with paraphrase proposals\\n\\nAdd paraphrase and topic-shift proposals. Allow the chain to discover related topics. Run for substantial time (10\\u2074-10\\u2075 samples).\\n\\nCompute: ~1000 hours; would benefit from a small GPU cluster.\\n\\n### Phase 4: Replica exchange\\n\\nAdd replica exchange across temperatures. Run multiple chains in parallel. Discover sharply-shaped vs softly-shaped regions.\\n\\nCompute: ~5x phase 3, but easily parallelized.\\n\\n### Phase 5: Cross-institutional comparison\\n\\nRun the full MCMC pipeline on multiple model pairs (Llama base/Instruct, Mistral base/Instruct, etc.). Compute differential signatures.\\n\\nCompute: linear in number of institutions.\\n\\n### Phase 6: Public methodology release\\n\\nDocument the methodology so other researchers can reproduce. Publish probe sets, MCMC code, analysis tools.\\n\\nThis is where the work has the most leverage \\u2014 once the methodology is reproducible by anyone with sufficient compute, it becomes infrastructure for institutional accountability.\\n\\n### Total scale\\n\\nA full execution of this program is comparable to a moderate physics computational study \\u2014 say, equilibrium statistical mechanics of a 3D Ising model with replica exchange. That's a feasible PhD-thesis or postdoc-project scale of work, not a one-person side project.\\n\\nThe framework's mathematical apparatus tells us this is the right kind of thing to do. The specific design above gives a concrete starting point. Whether anyone executes it is a separate question from whether it's possible.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Appendix: The literature\\n\\n**Sequential Monte Carlo for LLMs.** Zhao, Brekelmans, Makhzani, Grosse 2024 (\\\"Probabilistic Inference in Language Models via Twisted Sequential Monte Carlo\\\"). Casts RLHF, red-teaming, and related tasks as sampling from unnormalized distributions and applies SMC with learned twist functions. Provides bidirectional bounds on log partition functions \\u2014 directly relevant to estimating institutional shaping signatures.\\n\\n**MCMC for LLM probing.** Zhu, Yan, Griffiths 2024 (\\\"Recovering Mental Representations from Large Language Models with Markov Chain Monte Carlo\\\"). Uses MCMC to probe LLM representations more efficiently than direct prompting. Demonstrates significant efficiency gains over uniform prompting.\\n\\n**Curiosity-driven red teaming.** Hong et al. 2024 (\\\"Curiosity-driven Red-teaming for Large Language Models\\\"). Addresses the coverage problem in standard RL-based red teaming by adding novelty-seeking objectives. Relevant for designing proposal distributions that explore broadly.\\n\\n**Gradient-based attack methods.** GCG, AutoPrompt, and related work search prompt-space via gradients. These are deterministic alternatives to Monte Carlo with different trade-offs.\\n\\n**Physics Monte Carlo references.** Newman and Barkema \\\"Monte Carlo Methods in Statistical Physics\\\" (1999) for general background. Earl and Deem 2005 for replica exchange. Wang and Landau 2001 for the eponymous algorithm.\\n\\n---\\n\\n*End of document.*\\n\", \"filename\": \"/mnt/user-data/outputs/framework/monte_carlo_methods.md\"}"

    "text": "The Monte Carlo document is about 4,500 words, focused on the practical question. Key points worth highlighting:\n\n**Random literal prompts don't work.** This is the first thing to be clear about. Random token sequences are gibberish; the model's response to gibberish doesn't tell us about institutional shaping on contested topics. The \"rare event\" structure of physics Monte Carlo applies \u2014 the relevant configurations are tiny fraction of configuration space.\n\n**But structured Monte Carlo works.** The mathematical structure is the same as physics Monte Carlo. We have a function $V(x) = |\\bar{\\rho}(x)|$ on prompt-space, and we want to characterize where it's large. The tools are: importance sampling, Metropolis-Hastings MCMC, replica exchange, sequential Monte Carlo, free energy methods.\n\n**The key trade-off** is local vs broad exploration. Word-level perturbations preserve coherence but explore slowly. Paraphrase-via-LM moves faster but inherits the proposal model's biases. Topic-shifts are fastest but break detailed balance. A practical design mixes them with appropriate weights.\n\n**The fundamental limitation** is that LM-based proposals have their own institutional shaping \u2014 the proposal model's blind spots become our blind spots. Mitigations exist (multiple proposal models, base models, human-generated probes for sensitive categories) but don't fully resolve it.\n\n**The literature has the tools.** Zhao et al. 2024 on twisted SMC for LLM inference. Zhu, Yan, Griffiths 2024 on MCMC for LLM probing. Hong et al. 2024 on curiosity-driven red-teaming addressing coverage problems. All directly relevant to the framework's measurement program.\n\n**The compute scale** is research-program-sized. Phase E was a few hundred prompts on one GPU in hours. Full MCMC with replica exchange is potentially millions of inference calls \u2014 a small cluster, weeks to months of compute. Feasible as a PhD-thesis-level effort.\n\n**What you can't get**: the actual reward function (only $\\rho^*$, missing the per-prompt normalization $\\log Z(x)$); the training trajectory; per-token attribution; causal claims (only correlation between topic and shaping).\n\nSo the answer to \"can we learn about the model by throwing random prompts at it\" is: not random, but structured Monte Carlo on prompt-space recovers substantial information about the institutional shaping signature, and the math for doing this properly is well-developed in physics and now being adapted to LLMs.\n\nThe three documents now form a complete picture:\n- **probability_distortion_framework.md** \u2014 the math\n- **empirical_measurements.md** \u2014 what we measured on Llama and Claude\n- **monte_carlo_methods.md** \u2014 how to scale up the measurement methodology",

CMB and geodesy are literal siblings. SAR is a structural parallel. Seismology and structural monitoring are more distant cousins.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### Analysis Contributions (Claude)\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nThe forced Helmholtz formulation: $(-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Delta-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda_n)(M\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n) = [-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Delta,M]\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n$ with automatic solvability. The NSP resolution: for noiseless data with $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\mu_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\max} < 1$, exact recovery is trivial via direct inversion; $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\ell^1$ is a noise regularizer, not an identifiability tool. The commutator formula $s_{G,N} = [P_N, M_G]f = -\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\int_G \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Pi_N(\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\cdot,y)f(y)\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,dy$. The regime analysis (Regime III: high bandwidth recovers the uniform exact lie, not increased localization \\\\u2014 counterintuitive and correct). The boundary trace design rule: minimize $|\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n|_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial G}|^2$ as a second distinct rule. The three-basis structure: exposure, fabrication, ambiguity. The contrast kernel formula.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### Errors Corrected\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n- T6 was labeled [K] in an earlier version. Numerically verified (disk PDE cross-check): grid-based quadrature agrees with the $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\beta_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\pm}$ formula to 1.6\\\\u20132.8%, consistent with first-order Riemann sum error on a hard-edged mask.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n**[E] T3 \\\\u2014 Decomposition Theorem**\\\\\\\\n$$w_{G,N} = w_G + s_{G,N}.$$\\\\\\\\nExact for $f \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\in E_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\cap \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\operatorname{Ran} P_N$.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n**[E] T4 \\\\u2014 Boundary Wronskian Identity**\\\\\\\\n$$(\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda_m - \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda_n)K_{mn} = \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\int_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial G}[\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\bar{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi}_m\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\nu\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n - \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\nu\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\bar{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi}_m]\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,dS.$$\\\\\\\\nExact for $L = -\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Delta$ and smooth $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial G$. Follows from Green's second identity on $G$. *Note: the rectangular gap in \\\\u00a73.2 has corners where $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial G$ is not smooth; the Wronskian integral requires distributional treatment at corner points and T4 as stated does not apply directly there.*\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n**[E] T5 \\\\u2014 Masked Dictionary Gram Matrix**\\\\\\\\n$$\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\langle M_G\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n, M_G\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_m\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\rangle = A_{mn}.$$\\\\\\\\nExact for hard mask. $M_G^2 = M_G$ gives $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\langle M_G\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n, M_G\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_m\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\rangle = \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\langle M_G\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n, \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_m\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\rangle = A_{mn}$.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n**[O] T6 \\\\u2014 Boundary-Layer Localization**\\\\\\\\nFor $f \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\in E_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda$, the commutator correction $s_{G,N}$ concentrates in a tubular neighborhood of $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial G$ of width $O(\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda_N^{-1/2})$, with amplitude at $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial G$ controlled by $|f|_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial G}|$.\\\\\\\\n*Status: open.* Qualitatively correct from the formula $s_{G,N}(x) = -\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\int_G \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Pi_N(x,y)f(y)\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,dy$ and known decay of $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Pi_N$. The quantitative tube width and profile require H\\\\u00f6rmander's local Weyl law. The 1D Fourier case is proved explicitly. The general case is the missing formal theorem. *(An earlier version of the companion CCG document incorrectly marked this [K].)*\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n**[K] T7 \\\\u2014 Multipole Expansion**\\\\\\\\n$$K_{mn} = \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\sum_{|\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\alpha|\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\leq k} m_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\alpha\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial^\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\alpha(\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\bar{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi}_m)(x_G) + O(|G|\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,d_G^{k+1}).$$\\\\\\\\nFollows from Taylor's theorem. Eigenfunctions of elliptic operators are smooth on the interior.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n**[K] T8 \\\\u2014 Visibility-Rank for Degeneracies**\\\\\\\\nFor $d$-dimensional $E_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda$ and $d_G\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\sqrt{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda_N} \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\ll 1$: $B_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda$ has rank at most one at monopole order, and rank exactly one when $v_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\neq 0$. Follows from T7.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n**[O] T9 \\\\u2014 Multi-Gap Transversality Bound**\\\\\\\\n$\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\sigma_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\min}([A_1;\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\ldots;A_K])^2 \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\geq f(\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\theta_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\min})$ where $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\theta_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\min}$ is the minimum principal angle between the ambiguity bases.\\\\\\\\n*Status: open.* The geometric direction is correct. The explicit form of $f$ is the missing piece.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n--- Cell 21 ---\\\\\\\\n---\\\\\\\\n## Part XIII \\\\u2014 Development Record\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### What the Original Suite Had Right\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nThe exact energy identities T1 and T2 \\\\u2014 the suite's strongest results. The two-compression structure: $B_\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda$ (phantom energy) vs $C_N$ (conditioning). The masked dictionary observation (T5). The downstream propagation story: $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\hat{I} = AIA^*$, false coherence, false damping, temporal inheritance tests. The structural vs. accidental zero distinction in $K_{mn}$. The annulus and quantum graph examples.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### What the Original Suite Got Wrong or Was Unclear\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nConflated the exact outside-gap phantom (uniform, nondecaying) with the finite-band visible artifact (localized, Gibbs-like). The $w_G / s_{G,N}$ separation resolves this.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nThe scattering analogy document (`scattering_analogy.ipynb`) originally said the phantom field \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"does not satisfy a PDE.\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\" Wrong: $(-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Delta-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda_n)(M\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n) = [-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Delta,M]\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n$ is a forced Helmholtz equation. The phantom field satisfies a reduced forced PDE. What it does not do is carry new physical energy.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nThe finite-band localization theorem was presented as an open mystery. It is the Gibbs behavior of $s_{G,N} = -\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\int_G \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Pi_N(\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\cdot,y)f(y)\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,dy$ \\\\u2014 not mysterious, just not yet formally proved with explicit bounds.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\"Five communities solved the same equation\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\": overstated. CMB and geodesy are literal siblings. SAR is a structural parallel. Seismology and structural monitoring are more distant cousins.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### Analysis Contributions (Claude)\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nThe forced Helmholtz formulation: $(-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Delta-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\lambda_n)(M\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n) = [-\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Delta,M]\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n$ with automatic solvability. The boundary Wronskian identity T4. The Bernoulli asymmetry: $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\beta=1$ (complete occlusion) is safe; $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\beta=1/2$ is worst case. The NSP resolution: for noiseless data with $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\mu_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\max} < 1$, exact recovery is trivial via direct inversion; $\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\ell^1$ is a noise regularizer, not an identifiability tool. The rank-one asymptotic as local tomography: the gap interrogates the eigenfunction family through the evaluation vector $v^{(N)} = (\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_1(x_G),\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\ldots,\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_N(x_G))$.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### Analysis Contributions (ChatGPT)\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\nThe $w_G / s_{G,N} / w_{G,N}$ decomposition formalized as three distinct objects \\\\u2014 the most important single conceptual clarification in the entire discussion. The commutator formula $s_{G,N} = [P_N, M_G]f = -\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\int_G \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\Pi_N(\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\cdot,y)f(y)\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\,dy$. The regime analysis (Regime III: high bandwidth recovers the uniform exact lie, not increased localization \\\\u2014 counterintuitive and correct). The boundary trace design rule: minimize $|\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\varphi_n|_{\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\partial G}|^2$ as a second distinct rule. The three-basis structure: exposure, fabrication, ambiguity. The contrast kernel formula.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n### Errors Corrected\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n- T6 was labeled [K] in an earlier version.
